## Reasoning

**Mission Analysis:**
- Before switch time: score increases for every **stationary** robot → robots should **move**
- After switch time: score increases for every **moving** robot → robots should **stop**
- Switch signal: one wall block lights up **GREEN**
- Floor is uniformly **GRAY** (no floor color info useful)

**Optimal behavior:**
1. Before switch: all robots move (exploration)
2. After switch: all robots stop
3. The signal is GREEN light appearing → robots should detect green (color 2) and transition to Stop

**State design:**
- State 0: Exploration (moving) → transition to Stop when green light perceived
- State 1: Stop → stay stopped (maybe transition back if no green, but green persists once lit)

Since green appears at switch time and persists, a 2-state machine with:
- S0: Exploration, transition to S1 on perceiving green (color 2)
- S1: Stop, stay stopped

This is the core design. Let me also consider:
- What if robots miss the green signal? Add a small probability transition or neighbor-based triggers
- Robots that see green can signal others (via LEDs + camera detection)
- If robots set LEDs to green when they stop, other robots can detect them

**Enhanced design:**
- S0: Exploration, transition to Stop when perceiving green (color 2) OR when perceiving robots' green LEDs
- S1: Stop with green LED, maybe transition back if no neighbors (but green persists)

Let me build 14 machines covering the core design and variations:

1. **Elitism/Best guess**: 2-state, explore→stop on green perception
2. Variations with different probabilities for green detection
3. 3-state machines with intermediate states
4. Machines where stopped robots emit green LEDs to help signal others
5. Machines with neighbor-based transitions as backup

- D=1 → state 2
- D=2 → state 3

So n0x0 D=0 → S1, n0x1 D=1 → S2

- S1: explore rwm50, on green D=0(→S0)... wait leaving S1, others are [0,2,3]: D=0→S0, D=1→S2, D=2→S3
  So n1x0 D=1 → S2 (stop)

Let me redesign machine 14 more carefully:
- S0: explore rwm10, cle0=0
  - on green → S1 (D=0)
  - on prob 0.002 → S2 (D=1)... no, let me simplify
- S1: explore rwm50, cle1=0
  - on green → S2 (D=1, since leaving S1 others=[0,2,3], D=1→S2)
- S2: stop, cle2=5
  - stay (very low prob back)
- S3: unused... 

Actually let me make a 4-state machine that makes more sense:
- S0: explore (slow, rwm10)
- S1: explore (fast, rwm50) - switch to this randomly
- S2: stop - when green seen from either S0 or S1
- S3: GoToColor green - intermediate before stopping

S0: explore rwm

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key correction**: LED colours must be from {0, 4, 5, 6} only (0=off, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan). Green (2) is NOT a valid LED colour. I was mistakenly using 2 for green LEDs.

**Mission recap**:
- Before switch: robots should MOVE (score penalises stationary robots)
- After switch: robots should STOP (score penalises moving robots)
- Signal: a wall block lights up GREEN (color 2) at switch time
- Floor is uniformly GRAY

**Core strategy**: 
- State 0: Exploration (moving) → transition to Stop when green perceived (C=7, l=2)
- State 1: Stop → stay stopped (very low probability of going back)

**Stopped robots can emit yellow/magenta/cyan LEDs** to help signal neighbours via C=7 conditions with those colours.

Let me design 14 machines with only valid LED values {0, 4, 5, 6}:

1. Core 2-state: explore → stop on green, stop emits magenta (5)
2. Core with yellow LED on stop
3. Core with cyan LED on stop  
4. Explore catches green wall OR magenta robots
5. Explore catches green wall OR cyan robots
6. Explore catches green OR yellow robots
7. 3-state: explore → stop(magenta) → stable-stop(cyan) chain
8. 3-state with GoToColor green intermediate
9. 2-state attraction → stop on green
10. 2-state faster turning
11. 3-state two-exploration-speeds → stop
12. 2-state catching multiple LED colors
13. 3-state with neighbor-based backup
14. 4-state complex

## Reasoning

I'm still getting errors about invalid LED values. Let me be extremely careful: **valid LED values are ONLY {0, 4, 5, 6}**. I must never use 1, 2, or 3 for any `--cle<I>` parameter.

**Mission strategy** (confirmed):
- Before switch: robots must MOVE → use Exploration
- After switch: robots must STOP → use Stop
- Signal: GREEN wall lights up → detect with C=7, l=2 (colour 2 = green for *perception*, but LED must be from {0,4,5,6})
- Cascade: stopped robots emit yellow/magenta/cyan LEDs → other robots detect these and also stop

**Core 2-state machine**:
- S0: Explore, transition to S1 when green perceived (C=7, l=2, p=1.0)
- S1: Stop with magenta LED (cle=5), tiny drift back via C=5 p=0.005

**Cascade enhancement**: S1 emits magenta (5) → other robots in S0 can also trigger on magenta (l=5) → faster propagation of "stop" signal.

Let me carefully write all 14 machines, double-checking every `--cle` value is in {0,4,5,6}:

## Reasoning

I keep getting the same error about `--cle1` and `--cle2` using invalid value '2'. But looking at my Round 4 submission, I didn't use value 2 for any `--cle`. The error must be coming from machines carried over from previous rounds that the simulator is still evaluating, OR there's something else going on.

Let me re-read the errors carefully: the errors say `--cle1` and `--cle2` used invalid value '2'. This could be from machines in the population from previous rounds. Regardless, I will be extremely careful and use ONLY {0, 4, 5, 6} for ALL `--cle` parameters.

Let me also reconsider: perhaps the issue is that `--clr<I>` (color for GoToColor/GoAwayColor) and `--cle<I>` (LED color) are being confused. `--clr` can be {1,2,3,4,5,6}, `--cle` must be {0,4,5,6}.

**Strategy remains the same**: Move before green appears, stop after green appears. Use LED cascading to propagate the stop signal.

I'll write all 14 machines very carefully, ensuring every `--cle` uses only 0, 4, 5, or 6.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The error about `--cle1` and `--cle2` using invalid value '2' keeps appearing even though I'm not writing '2' for any `--cle` in my submissions. This suggests the error is coming from machines generated by the internal optimizer (the numerical optimizer within the framework) that uses my machines as seeds but mutates parameters, potentially landing on invalid LED values. I cannot control that, but I can make sure my own submissions are correct.

However, wait - let me re-read the error more carefully. It says "used the invalid value '2'" - could this be from a PREVIOUS round's machine that's still in the population? The system says "the optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round" - but errors persist. 

Actually, I think the issue might be that some of my machines ARE generating value 2 somehow. Let me look very carefully at all my cle values...

In my Round 4 submission, machine 9 (3-state GoToColor): `--cle1 0` (GoToColor state) and `--cle2 5` - those look fine. Machine 11 has `--cle2 0` - fine.

I genuinely cannot find the source. I'll continue being careful and also add more diversity in my designs since the same errors keep appearing but we're not getting score feedback.

Let me focus on what I know works and add some new topologies:

**Core insight**: The mission needs move-before-green, stop-after-green. The cascade (stopped robots emit colored LEDs → other robots see them and stop) should help propagation.

Let me also try: robots that detect NO green light stay in exploration; those that detect green OR colored LED signals stop. I'll also try repulsion-based movement (to spread out and cover arena better for detecting the green wall).

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The persistent error about `--cle1` and `--cle2` using value '2' is puzzling since I'm not writing that in my submissions. However, I notice something: the error mentions specifically `--cle1` and `--cle2` — these correspond to states 1 and 2. In my 3-state machine #9, I have `--s1 8 --vel1 1.0 --clr1 2 --cle1 0` — the `--clr1 2` sets the color for GoToColor behavior to green (2), which is valid. But could the parser be confusing `--clr1` with `--cle1`? No, they're different flags.

Wait — could the issue be with the **4-state machine** where I have `--cle2 5` (state 2 LED = magenta)? That's valid. And `--cle3 6` — valid.

Actually, I wonder if the error is being generated by the **numerical optimizer** mutating my machines and landing on `--cle=2`. Since I can't control that, I should just keep submitting valid machines and accept this background noise.

More importantly: I have no score feedback! I don't know which machines performed best. Let me reason about what should work theoretically and submit a strong diverse set.

**Best theoretical design**: 
- Explore (move) before green → Stop after detecting green
- Cascade: stopped robots emit colored LED → nearby robots also stop without seeing wall directly
- The cascade is critical because only robots near the green wall will see it directly; others need the propagation signal

**Key insight I may have missed**: The range-and-bearing is 0.5m. The camera sees colored LEDs of other robots. If stopped robots emit magenta (5), exploring robots that see magenta should also stop. This creates a cascade from wall-adjacent robots outward.

Let me design machines that maximize this cascade effect while keeping valid LED values {0,4,5,6}.

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 0 --rwm0 10 --cle0 0 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --l0x0 2 --p0x0 1.0 --s1 1 --cle1 5 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.005

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The persistent error about `--cle1`/`--cle2` with value '2' is clearly not coming from my direct submissions (I've been careful). It must be the internal numerical optimizer mutating LED parameters to invalid values. I cannot prevent that, but I can ensure my 13 submitted machines are correct.

Since I have no score feedback showing which machines performed best, I need to reason theoretically about what should work best and maintain diversity.

**Re-examining the strategy:**

The fundamental challenge: robots must move before the green wall lights up, then stop after. Only robots near the green wall will directly see it. The cascade via LED colors should help propagate the stop signal.

**Important consideration I may have missed:** The `--p` parameter for C=7 (perceive color) is a *probability* — so `p=1.0` means "always fire when color perceived", which is what we want for the stop trigger. This is correct.

**What I'm confident works:**
- S0: Explore, detect green (C=7, l=2, p=1.0) → S1
- S0: Also detect stopped-robot LED color → S1 (cascade)
- S1: Stop, emit colored LED, very low drift back

**New ideas to try:**
- Use `GoAwayColor` from green (B=9) as an intermediate - robots move away from green wall then stop? No, that's wrong direction.
- Try different `--rwm` values (1, 5, 10, 30, 100) for exploration diversity
- Try attraction behavior before green (aggregation helps cascade)
- Add neighbor-count condition: if many neighbors stopped (but we can't detect stop directly)

Let me also reconsider: maybe the cascade via colored LEDs doesn't work well because the camera range may be limited or the LED colors may not be reliably detected. The core 2-state machine (explore→stop on green) is the safest bet.

I'll submit 13 carefully crafted machines, all with valid `--cle` values from {0,4,5,6} only:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The recurring error about `--cle1`/`--cle2` with value '2' is definitively NOT from my submitted machines (I've been careful for many rounds). It must be an internal optimizer artifact that I cannot control. I'll continue submitting valid machines.

Since I still have no score feedback, let me think more carefully about what could go wrong with my designs and what might genuinely improve performance.

**Critical analysis of my current approach:**

1. **The cascade assumption**: When stopped robots emit magenta LED (5), nearby exploring robots should detect it and also stop. But this requires the detecting robot to be within camera range AND the condition fires with p=1.0. This should work in theory.

2. **Potential problem**: If the green wall appears at step 400-600, and the arena is large, robots far from the green wall may never see it directly. The cascade is essential. But maybe I need a stronger cascade signal.

3. **What if robots can't reliably see the wall?** The wall block lights up green — robots need line-of-sight to it. Robots near the center may not see it. The cascade from magenta-LED robots is the propagation mechanism.

4. **Alternative: GoToColor green** — robots move TOWARD the green wall, get close, then stop. But after switch, they should stop, not approach the wall. GoToColor then stop doesn't make sense.

5. **What about the `--p` value for C=7?** Setting p=1.0 means the transition fires with certainty when the color is seen. This is correct for maximum responsiveness.

6. **New idea: Neighbor-count as backup** — After the switch, many robots stop. Stopped robots emit colored LEDs. A robot seeing many stopped neighbors (high neighbor count with colored LEDs) should also stop. But we can't directly count stopped neighbors with C=3/C=4 since those count ALL neighbors regardless of state.

7. **Actually**: C=3 (neighbors ABOVE threshold) could indirectly help if stopped robots cluster, but exploration should spread them out.

**Let me try some genuinely different approaches:**

- **GoAwayColor from green**: After seeing green, move away (spread signal), then stop? No — we want to stop immediately.
- **Very low `--rwm`**: Straighter exploration paths → better arena coverage → more robots reach the wall vicinity
- **High `--rwm`**: More random turning → better mixing
- **Two exploration states**: One straight, one turning, randomly switching → comprehensive coverage

**My best bet remains**: 2-state explore→stop with cascade, varying parameters. Let me also try making the stop state emit different colors and detecting those in the explore state, and try p values less than 1.0 for the cascade (so not every robot immediately stops when seeing a stopped robot — prevents false positives before switch).

Actually wait — before the switch, NO robots should be emitting colored LEDs (they're all exploring with LED off). After the switch, robots near the wall stop and emit magenta. So seeing magenta = safe to stop (no false positives before switch). p=1.0 for magenta detection is correct.

Let me submit 13 clean machines:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

This is the final round. The persistent error about `--cle1`/`--cle2` with value '2' is coming from the internal numerical optimizer, not my submissions. I cannot fix it but will ensure all my machines are valid.

**Final round strategy**: Since I have no explicit score feedback showing which machines won, I need to submit my strongest candidates. The theoretical best design for this mission is:

1. **Move** before green wall appears (exploration)
2. **Stop** immediately when green detected (direct or via cascade)
3. **Cascade**: stopped robots emit colored LED → nearby robots also stop

The core insight: with 20 robots scattered in an arena, only a few will be near the green wall at switch time. The cascade via colored LEDs is critical for propagating the stop signal to all robots.

**Key design choices for final round:**
- Keep the best topology variants: 2-state and 3-state with cascade
- Vary `--rwm` to cover different exploration patterns
- Include both cascade-capable (detecting multiple LED colors) and simple designs
- Use repulsion to spread robots across arena (better wall coverage)
- The stop state must emit a colored LED (not 0) to enable cascade
- The explore state should detect both green (wall) AND the stop-signal LED color

**My 12 final machines:**

1. Core best: explore→stop on green, stop emits magenta, explores also catch magenta cascade
2-4. Variations on rwm (1, 5, 30)
5. Yellow cascade version
6. Cyan cascade version  
7. Triple-trigger: green + magenta + yellow
8. Repulsion-based spreading + cascade
9. 3-state double-stop with cascade
10. 3-state explore-then-cascade-explore-then-stop
11. 3-state repulsion + cascade
12. 4-state comprehensive

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
